RoE d. DODSON v. GREW. 1767. (2 Wils. 322.) 

Facts: Devise to A. for life, remainder to the issue male of his body; in default of such issue male, remainder to B. 

Question: Does A. take an estate for life or in tail?

Held: Although the remainder is limited to the issue of 

A., it clearly appears from the context that the intention was to give in succession to all the sons of A., i. e. to the heirs male of his body. Consequently, Shelley's Case applies, and A takes an estate tail. 

Proposition: Where it appears from the context that a remainder is limited in succession to the heirs of the ancestor to whom a prior estate of freehold has been given, Shelley's Case applies, even though some term other than "heirs," as "issue" or "children," be used to designate those to take in remainder.

PERRIN V. BLAKE. 1769. (1 W. Bl. 672.) Facts: The will contained the following recital: “It is my intent and meaning that none of my children shall sell or dispose of my estate for a longer term than his life, and to that intent, I devise all my property to my son A., and any son my wife may be enceinte of at my death, for their natural lives; remainder to the heirs of the body of my said sons, A. and the said infant lawfully to be begotten. At the testator's death, A. and the wife were living; but the wife was not enceinte. 

Question: Does A. take an estate for life or in tail?

Held: A majority of the Court of King's Bench held that the expressed intention was to prevail, and that A.

took merely an estate for life. On error to the Exchequer 

Chamber, the decision of King's Bench was reversed by a large majority of the judges. By further writ of error, the case was carried to the House of Lords, where it remained pending several years. But before any hearing had been had, the parties effected a compromise, and terminated the action.

The fact that the Exchequer Chamber, in the face of the express declaration of the testator that A. should not be able to dispose of his estate for longer than his life, nevertheless held that under the Rule in Shelley's Case he took an estate tail, gives the following proposition.

Proposition: Once it is settled, on the construction of a devise, that a remainder is limited to the heirs of the ancestor to whom a prior estate of freehold has been given, the rule automatically applies, and no expression of an intention to exclude it can prevail. 21 See Fearne, C. R. 171-173.

JESSON v. WRIGHT. 1820. (2 Bligh, 1.) 

Facts: Devise to A. for life, remainder to the heirs of his body, in such shares and proportions as he should appoint; and for want of such appointment, to the heirs of his body, share and share alike, as tenants in common; and if but one child, all to such child; and for want of such issue, then over. 

Question: Does A. take an estate for life or in tail? 

Held: The testator, by his use of the words "heirs of 

21 "The application of the rule in Shelley's Case to wills is independent of any expressions of an intention which do not enter into and affect the limitations upon which it operates. Intention rules and controls the separate limitations; but it cannot prevent or reach the legal consequences resulting from the limitations used.”- -1 Leake, 258. And see Daniel v. Whartenby, 17 

Wall. 643; Fearne, C. R. 171–173.

the body" in the limitation of the remainder, has evinced an intention that all the issue of A. should fail before the estate should go over according to the final limitation. 

It is true that he has also employed other expressions (such as, "in default of appointment, to the heirs of the body of A., as tenants in common;" "if but one child;" and "for want of such issue") which point to a gift rather to the children of A., than to the heirs as a class embracing the line of inheritable succession. But since these inconsistent expressions do not, of necessity, indicate that the testator did not mean to use the technical words "heirs of the body" in their proper sense, Shelley's Case applies, and A. takes an estate tail.

Proposition: Where, after a devise of a freehold to the ancestor, the technical words "heirs of the body" are used to designate those to take in remainder, the rule invariably applies, unless, from other expressions, it is patent that the testator did not intend the technical words to have their legal effect.22 

22 GENERAL AND PARTICULAR INTENT IN CONNECTION WITH THE RULE 

IN SHELLEY'S CASE.-At the outset, the rule was regarded as one to discover intention; and in dealing with such a case as the present, the Court would endeavor to divide the intention into two parts, one of which would warrant the application of the rule, and the other, would not. The former was called the general, the latter, the particular intent; and the application of the rule was sought to be justified on the ground that the particular intent must give way to the general. (See Lord Eldon's opinion in principal case.) "The consequence was an unspeakable quagmire, out of which escape was finally had only by the total repudiation of the theory of general and particular intent, and by a firm grasp on the principle that the object of the Rule is to defeat intention."-Gray, Perp. § 882. ... "That the general intent should overrule the particular, is not the most accurate expression of the principle of decision. The rule is, that technical words shall have their legal effect, unless, from subsequent inconsistent words, it is very clear that the testator meant otherwise."-Per Lord Redesdale, in principal case, 2 Bligh, 56, 57. And see note, p. 46, supra; Clark v. Smith, 49 Md. 117; DeVaughn v. Hutchinson, 165 U. S. 575; Hileman v. 

Bouslaugh, 13 Pa. St. 351.

JORDAN v. ADAMS. 1861. 

Facts: Devise to A. for life, remainder to the heirs of his body, in such shares as A., their father, should direct. 

Question: Does A. take an estate for life or in tail? 

Held: The direction of the testator, following immediately on the gift to the heirs, that they should take in such shares as A., their father should direct, indicates that the word "heirs" was employed, not in its technical sense, to denote a line of inheritable succession, but rather in its popular sense, namely, that of sons, daughters, or children, as the case may be. Since Shelley's Case applies only when the word "heirs" is used in its technical sense, A. takes but an estate for life. 

Proposition: Where, after a devise of a freehold to the ancestor, the technical words "heirs of the body" are used to designate those to take in remainder, the rule invariably applies, unless from other expressions it is patent that the testator did not intend the technical words to have their legal effect. 

SUMMARY 

1. Where a person takes an estate of freehold by deed, will, or other writing, and in the same instrument there is a limitation by way of remainder to his heirs, as a class of persons to take in succession, from generation to generation, the heirs are words of limitation of the estate, and not words of purchase. Shelley's Case, p. 39. 

2. The effect of the rule is to attribute to the remainder the force of a gift to the ancestor himself, just as though the limitation had originally been to him for life, with remainder to himself and his heirs. 1 Hayes, Conv. 543, 545.

3. For the rule to apply, the limitations to the ancestor and the heirs must be by the same instrument. Moor v. 

Parker, p. 44. 

3. The rule applies to equitable as well as legal limitations. Baile v. Coleman, p. 44. 

4. The rule applies, even though the remaindermen are referred to by some term other than "heirs," such as "issue" or "children," where the context shows the remainder to be limited in succession to the heirs of the ancestor to whom a prior estate of freehold has been given. Roe d. Dodson v. Grew, p. 45. 

5. And where, after a devise of a freehold to the ancestor, the technical words "heirs of the body" are used to designate those to take in remainder, the rule invariably applies, unless from other expressions it is patent that the technical words were not meant to be used in their ordinary sense, but instead to designate some particular person or persons to whom the estate was intended to pass, after the death of the first devisee. Perrin v. 

Blake, p. 45; Jesson v. Wright, p. 46; Jordan v. Adams, p. 48. 

6. Such intention appears when, after a devise of a freehold to the ancestor and a remainder to his heir, fresh words of limitation are superadded, whereby a new inheritance is engrafted upon the heir to whom the remainder is limited. Archer's Case, p. 43.

CHAPTER IV FUTURE USES INTRODUCTION Future Uses Before the Statute of Uses: At common law, the only way a freehold could be created to commence in futuro was by way of remainder after a previously vested estate of freehold. No such technical rule obtained in equity, however; and uses were always allowed to be limited in futuro, regardless as to whether or not they were preceded by any particular estate. Thus, if A. enfeoffed B. to hold to the use of C. and his heirs after the first day of May next ensuing, on the arrival of the appointed day the Chancellor would compel the feoffee to carry out the use. It was also possible in equity to limit a fee after a fee by way of use, as, for instance, by a conveyance to A. and his heirs so long as he should remain unmarried, and upon his marriage to the use of B. and his heirs. This facility which Chancery afforded for creating future interests in land was an important factor in promoting the growth of the use before the enactment of the above-mentioned statute.¹

Future Uses After the Statute: The Statute of Uses, 

27 Hen. VIII. c. 10 (1535), invested the cestui que use with the legal as well as the beneficial estate, i. c. made the legal estate vest wherever the use vested. Accordingly, since there was no restriction in equity upon the creation of uses in futuro, it became possible, after the statute, to 

¹ Digby, Hist. Real Prop. 330, 331; 4 Kent, Comm. 291, 293. 50

FUTURE USES 

51 limit a freehold interest to take effect in possession at a future time other than by way of remainder.² 

Classification of Future Uses: Future uses are classified as (1) shifting, (2) springing, and (3) future or contingent.³

A shifting use arises, where, after a use has been properly created and has vested, the interest under the use shifts to another on the occurrence of a specified event.¹ As, if a grant be to A. and his heirs to the use of B. and his heirs, but if B. die in A.'s lifetime, then to the use of C. and his heirs, upon the death of B. in the lifetime of A. the use is said to shift to C.

A springing use is a future use which takes effect de novo,—that is, when it springs up it cuts short no other express existing use. If A. covenant to stand seized to the use of B. after ten years, or if land be conveyed to A. in fee to the use of B. in fee after next Christmas, these are examples of springing uses.

Future or Contingent Uses are uses which take effect on the determination of preceding estates created at the same time, or, in other words, are uses limited by way of remainder." 

Digby, Hist. Real Prop. 357; 4 Kent, Comm. 294, 296. * Gilbert, Uses (Sugd. ed.), 152, 153, note. 

4 Ibid., 152, note; Digby, Hist. Real Prop. 358. 

5 Gilbert, Uses (Sugd. ed.), 153, note; 4 Kent, Comm. 297, 298. • Gilbert, Uses (Sugd. ed.), 153, note; Gray, Perp. § 54.

NOTE ON CONTINGENT USES: Contingent uses were subject to precisely the same rules as contingent remainders; and by a series of decisions it became established that if a conveyancer, in attempting to create a shifting or springing use, expressed the condition upon which the use was to arise in language applicable to the creation of a remainder, the limitation would be regarded as a contingent use, and its validity tested by the technical requirements of the common law. Thus, while a feoffment to the use of 

C. in fee after the death of A. and B. was a springing use, a feoffment to the use of A. for life, and after the death of A. and B., to the use of C. in fee, would be considered a contingent remainder to C., although the use might be defeated by the death of A. in the life of B. Weale v. Lower, Pollexf. 65;

CASES ADAMS v. SAVAGE. 1703. (2 Ld. Raym. 854.) 

Facts: A., seized in fee, conveys land to trustees to the use of himself for ninety-nine years, if he should so long live, remainder to trustees for twenty-five years, remainder to the heirs male of his body, remainder to his own right heirs.

Question: A. being childless, is the contingent remainder to the heirs male of his body good after an estate for years? 

Held: The contingent remainder is bad because not preceded by an estate of freehold. No freehold is expressly limited to A.; and none will be implied by way of resulting use, because he has conveyed the entire estate to uses, and a use will not be raised by implication in derogation of an express use.

Proposition: A use limited by way of contingent remainder is void, unless preceded by an estate of freehold." 

Gilbert, Uses (Sugd. ed.), 172, n. And the circumstance that a limitation, void in its creation as a remainder, would be valid if construed as a springing use, was utterly disregarded in applying this principle. Adams v. Savage, p. 52. uses.

Modern statutes, however, have materially altered the law of contingent In England, by St. 40 & 41 Vict. c. 33 (1877), it is provided that a limitation which would have been valid as a springing or shifting use had not a preceding estate enabled it to take effect as a contingent remainder, shall be capable of taking effect in all respects as a springing or shifting use on determination of the particular estate before it has vested. In New 

York, under the Revised Statutes, contingent remainders limited by way of use are converted into legal contingent remainders; and no valid contingent remainder can be defeated by the premature determination of the particular estate. See N. Y. Consol. Laws, Real Prop. Law, §§ 58, 91, 93, 96. 

7 That a future contingent devise after an estate for years is a good executory devise, and not a void remainder, is settled. Gore v. Gore, p. 67,

FUTURE USES 

53 

ROGERS V. EAGLE FIRE COMPANY. 1832. (9 Wend. 611.) 

Facts: A., by bargain and sale, conveys property to B. and his heirs in fee, after the death of the grantor. 

Question: Is such limitation valid? 

Held: A future use can be raised by a covenant to stand seized; and no good reason exists why the same uses may not be created by bargain and sale, founded upon a valuable consideration, as are allowed to be raised by the less meritorious consideration of blood or marriage. 

Proposition: A future freehold can be raised by bargain and sale.

SUMMARY 

1. Since a contingent use preceded by an estate for years is valid (Adams v. Savage, p. 52, contra, seems no longer law). 

2. Since a future freehold can be raised by bargain and sale,-Rogers v. Eagle Fire Company, supra. 

3. Virtually the only existing restraint on the creation of future estates in land by way of use is the Rule against 

Perpetuities. Gray, Perp. §§ 55–66. 

Gray, Perp. §§ 55-66. See pp. 54, 200, infra. infra; Gilbert, Uses (Sugd. ed.), 168, n. On principle, then, it would seem that a future contingent use, similarly limited, should be given effect as a springing use, instead of being construed as a remainder void in its creation. 

And it has been suggested that the only reason why Adams v. Savage has not been expressly overruled is because the point has not arisen under deeds, as it has, under wills. Gray, Perp. §§ 59, 60; and see art., 1 Law 

Quart. Rev. 412, by Mr. H. W. Challis.

CHAPTER V  INTRODUCTION 

It was pointed out in the chapter preceding that the Rule against Perpetuities is virtually the only existing restraint on the creation of future estates in land by way of use.¹ A study of the following cases will disclose that the development of the law has steadily tended toward the elimination of all restrictions, other than with respect to remoteness, on the creation of future interests in personalty, as well. So far has this development progressed that it may now be said of future interests in personalty, as of future estates in land by way of use, that substantially the only check on their creation is the Rule against 

Perpetuities.2 CASES FITZ-JAMES'S CASE. 1565. (Owen, 33.) 

Facts: Bequest of plate to the use of A., and the heirs of his body. 

Question: Does A. take any legal right of property? 

Held: Under this bequest, A. acquires no legal right of property, but merely a right of use and occupation for life. 

¹ Supra, p. 53. 

2 Gray, Perp. §§ 69, 98; and see 30 Cyc. 1467, n. 1; also note, p. 61, infra. 54

Proposition: At common law, a bequest of the use and occupation of personalty for life was valid, but conveyed no legal right of ownership or property.³

MANNING'S CASE. 1609. (8 Co. 94b.) 

Facts: Bequest of a term to A. for life, and after her death to B. 

Question: Is the gift over to B. valid?

Held: Although the term is given to A. for life, on her death it shall vest in B. as by an executory bequest. 

And it is immaterial whether the gift to A. is of the term or of the use of the term.

Proposition: A bequest of a chattel real to A. for life, and after his death to B. is good, the gift over taking effect as an executory bequest.¹ 

*NOTE ON FUTURE INTERESTS IN PERSONALTY AT COMMON LAW: (a) Gifts by Will.-At the outset, the only legal interest which could be created by will in chattels, either real or personal, was an absolute one. See articles in 3 Harv. Law Rev. 315, 316, by Professor Ames; in 14 Harv. Law Rev. 

408, by Professor Gray. But the use and occupation in a chattel could be given for life; and while, as above held, such bequest gave no legal right of property, it did give the use and enjoyment. (b) Gifts Inter Vivos.-At the outset the only interest which could be created inter vivos in a chattel personal was likewise an absolute one. Bro. 

Abr. Devise, 13-"a gift or devise of a chattel is forever." While it has also been stated that "the gift of a term (like any other chattel) for an hour was good forever"—per Lord Mansfield, in Wright v. Cartwright, 1 Burr. 

282, 284-nevertheless, from a very early date an estate for years in a chattel real could be created by sub-lease. 2 P. & M. Hist. Eng. Law, 112. 

• EXECUTORY BEQUESTS: (a) Theory upon which Executory Bequest of 

Term was Sustained.—It being a presumption of law that an estate for life is greater than one for years, a gift of a term for life passes the entire property. But there is no presumption that a particular individual will not die within a term: so, to sustain the limitation over, the Court here simply reversed the order of gifts, construing the limitation "to A. for life, and after her death to B.," as though the testator had first bequeathed the term to 

B. after the death of A. (a stranger), and had then given it to A., subject

Facts: Bequest of a term to A. for life, and after his death to his eldest issue male for life. At the time of the devise and death of the testator, A. was without issue male.

Question: Is such executory bequest for life, to a person not in esse, good? 

Held: If A. has issue male to take at his death, such issue will be entitled by way of executory bequest. 

Proposition: An executory bequest of a term to one for life, and after his death to an unborn person for life, is valid. 4 Kent, Comm. 269.

ANONYMOUS. 1641. (March, 106, pl. 183.) 

Facts: Bequest of chattels personal to A. for life, and after her death, to B. 

Question: Does B. acquire any interest under this bequest?

Held: A gift of chattels personal to B., after a bequest of the use and occupation to A. for life, would have been to such executory bequest. Vide Point 1, resolved in principal case; Lewis, Perp. 87; Gray, Perp. §§ 807-815. As to limitations inter vivos, see Wright v. Cartwright, p. 58, infra, note (b). 

(b) Doctrine of Executory Bequests Not Extended to Chattels Personal.—An early exception to the common-law rule that "a devise of a chattel is forever" was established by Manning's Case with respect to chattels real, viz. upon a bequest to A. for life, the whole term is considered to vest in him until death, and then to pass, by way of executory bequest, to the person next entitled. Although subsequently, a bequest of a chattel personal to 

B., after a life interest to A., also came to be upheld, yet this was upon the theory that A. is to be regarded as having merely the use and occupation, and B. the immediate vested right of property. Hide v. Parrat, p. 57, infra; see Williams, Pers. Prop. 357, 358; Gray, Perp. §§ 86a, 854, 855.

valid; for B.'s interest under such gift would be all one with that of the owner of goods in pawn, to which the pawnee has the temporary right of possession. But where the goods are themselves bequeathed, there can be no gift over.

Proposition: A gift of chattels personal to one, after a bequest of them for life to another, is bad; although such gift would have been good, had the prior bequest been of the use and occupation only.5 

HIDE V. PARRAT. 1696. (2 Vern. 331.) 

Facts: Bequest of goods to A. for life, and after her death to B., forever. 

Question: Is the gift over to B. good?

Held: The gift over is good: for where chattels personal are bequeathed for a limited time, it shall be intended as a gift of the use of them only, and not of the property itself.

Proposition: If a chattel personal is bequeathed to A. for life, and on his death to B., the bequest to A. will be considered as a gift of the use of the chattel only, and the property itself will be considered to belong to B.6 

EYRES v. FAULKLAND. 1697. (1 Salk. 231.) 

Facts: Bequest of a term of ninety-nine years to A. for life, to B. for life, and so on to five others successively for life. All seven die within the term. 

5 But this distinction between a gift over of a chattel personal after a prior bequest of the use and occupation, and of the chattel itself, was in the latter part of the century repudiated. See Hide v. Parrat, post; Fearne, C. R. 406, 407; Lewis, Perp. 96; Gray, Perp. § 84. 

Accord., Westcott v. Cady, 5 Johns. Ch. 334; Johnson's Admr. v. Johnson, 104 Ky. 714; Philips v. Crews, 65 Ca. 274. See 2 Kent, Comm. 352.

Question: To whom does the term pass?

Held: The term passes to the executor of the testator. 

Proposition: If a term is bequeathed to A. for life, and there is no express gift over, on the death of A. the term passes to the executor of the testator."

WRIGHT v. CARTWRIGHT. 1757. (1 Burr. 282.) 

Facts: Demise to A. for ninety-nine years if she should so long live; and after her death, if she happen to die within the said term, the remainder thereof to B. her eldest son for and during the residue of the said term; yielding and paying, etc., and doing suit at a mill, etc.; with a penalty for every time that A. or B. shall grind at another mill; and paying a heriot on the death of either. And it is covenanted that both of them shall repair; and the lessor on his part covenants that both shall quietly enjoy, etc. 

A. dies within the term. 

Question: Does B. take any interest under the demise?

Held: The old cases held that there could be no limitation of a term after an estate for life, by deed or will. But it came to be recognized that such limitation by will would be effectuated as an executory bequest; and the same reasons which led to the upholding of these limitations by will required that they might also be created by deed. So, to escape the literal authority of the old cases, an ingenious distinction was invented: a remainder might be limited for the residue of the years, but not for the residue of the term.

A bequest of a term for life passes the entire property: any gift over must be by way of executory limitation. There being here no limitation over after the death of the last surviving life tenant, it is difficult to see why the term did not pass to his, instead of the testator's executor. Gray, Perp. §§ 819, 820; but see Fearne, C. R. 488.

Here the limitation is, upon A.'s death within the term, to B. for and during the residue of the term. The word term may signify, not only the estate or interest, but also the time or number of years. In this case, it must be taken in the latter sense; for the plain intention that B. is to take upon his mother's death (indicated by the provisions that he is to repair, to pay a penalty for grinding at another mill, to pay a heriot on her death; and by the covenant that he shall quietly enjoy) can be supported only by construing the limitation as a gift to him for so many of the ninety-nine years as should be unexpired at her death. 2 Proposition: Although it is impossible, at common law, to grant a term to one for life, and the residue of the term to another, nevertheless, if the court can construe a demise as comprising a gift to one for so much of the term of years as he should live, and a gift to another, to commence at the death of the first taker, of an estate for so many of the years as then remain, the latter gift will be upheld as a limitation in futuro of the residue of the years. 1 Leake, 166. 

HOARE v. PARKER. 1788. (2 T. R. 376.) 

Facts: Bequest of plate to the use of A. for life, and after her death to B. After A.'s death, B. brings trover for the plate against the defendant, with whom A. had pawned it. 

8 (a) There being no seizin of a chattel real, it was possible to sustain the limitation to B. as a grant in futuro, for the residue of the years. 2 Bl. 

Comm. 143, 144; 1 Tiffany, Real Prop. § 146. (b) Here we have indication of the avidity with which courts avail of a construction to avoid the doctrine that a term cannot be limited inter vivos to A. for life, and after his death to B. Gray, Perp. § 812, note 5; 

Williams, Pers. Prop. 356. In America, although there seems no decision exactly in point, it is probable that, following the analogy of chattels personal, life interests in chattels real will be allowed to be created by deed as well as will. Gray, Perp. §§ 71b, 853.

Question: Can B. maintain trover (an action implying a legal right to possession)?

Held: Even though the defendant advanced the money on the plate in ignorance of A.'s limited property, B. can recover.

Proposition: On a bequest of a chattel personal to the use of A. for life, and then to B., after A.'s death B. has a legal right to possession sufficient to sustain trover. 

IN RE TRITTON. 1889. (6 Morrell, Bankr. Cas. 250.) 

Facts: Bequest of certain pictures to B. after the death of A., to whom the enjoyment of them for life is given. 

Question: Has B. any property in the pictures during the life of A.?

Held: Under the bequest B.'s interest is wholly executory, A. having the entire property while she lives. 

Proposition: On a bequest of a chattel personal to A. for life, and after his death to B., A. has the entire property for life, which passes at his death to B.9 

ANONYMOUS. 1802. [2 Hayw. (N. Car.) 161.] 

Facts: Bequest of a chattel personal to A. for life. 

Question: At A.'s death, who is entitled to the chattel, the executor of A. or of the testator?

Held: The executor of the testator is entitled. 

Proposition: If a chattel personal is bequeathed to A. for life, with no express gift over, on A.'s death the chattel reverts to the executor of the testator. 'The theory of this case, while inconsistent with the earlier decisions (see Hide v. Parrat, p. 57, supra), is favored by the modern English text writers. Fearne, C. R. 401, Butler's Note; 2 Prest. Abst. 144; Lewis, Perp. 

97; and see Gray, Perp. §§ 86a, 833, 854.

DUKE V. DYCHES. 1829. (2 Strob. Eq. 353n.) 

Facts: By deed of gift, A. gives certain slaves to B., reserving a life interest in them to himself. 

Question: At A.'s death, who is entitled to the slaves 

A.'s executor or B.?

Held: The rule in England and America is that a gift of a chattel personal to one for life, and on his death to another, is valid when made by will. As no good reason exists why such gift should not also be allowed by deed, B. is entitled to the slaves.

Proposition: A legal interest in a chattel personal after a gift for life to another can be created by deed as well as will; hence, a gift inter vivos of such chattel, to become effective on the donor's death, is valid.¹0

BRUMMET v. BARBER. 1834. [2 Hill (S. Car.), 543.] 

Facts: The donor, by writing not under seal, gives certain slaves to A. for life, and on his death to B. After the death of A., who has had the use of the slaves for life, they are claimed by B. 

Question: Is B. entitled? 

Held: B. is entitled to the slaves.

Proposition: A gift inter vivos of a chattel personal to A. for life, and after his death to B., is good, even though by parol, where delivery accompanies the gift to the first taker. 11 

10 In the United States (with the exception of North Carolina), the principal case is generally followed. But in England and North Carolina, it seems that no limitation over of a chattel personal after a grant for life can be made at law. Gray, Perp. §§ 78, 92, 854; 30 Cyc. 1467. 

11 This is not law in England or North Carolina. See preceding note.

ROGERS v. RANDALL. 1842. (2 Speers, 38.) 

Facts: Bequest of negroes to A., and, if A. should die unmarried and without issue, then to B. A. sold one of the slaves to the defendant, who had no notice of the limitations in the will. Thereafter, A. having died unmarried and without issue, B. brings trover for the slave. 

Question: Can B. recover? 

Held: B. is entitled to the slave.

Proposition: An executory limitation over after an absolute bequest of a chattel personal is a valid legal interest. 

WILSON V. COCKRILL. 1843. (8 Mo. 1.) 

Facts: A., by deed, gives certain slaves to B., and other slaves to C., on condition, however, that if either B. or C. should die without heirs, the slaves of the one so dying should belong to the other. B. dies without heirs. 

Question: Is C. entitled to the slaves?

Held: C. is not entitled. The legal right of property in the slaves having been given absolutely to B., the executory gift over is bad, although (semble) it might have been valid if made by will or conveyance under the Statute of Uses.

Proposition: If an absolute interest in a chattel personal is given by deed, an executory gift over is void. 12 

12 But in other States (except North Carolina and Kentucky) an executory gift over after an absolute interest can be made by deed as well as by will. 

Gray, Perp. §§ 95, 847. 

CONSUMABLE CHATTELS: A gift of articles quæ ipso usu consumuntur, as wines, etc., for life, or for a year, passes the entire property. Randall v. 

Russell, 3 Meriv. 190; Williams, Pers. Prop. 359; 2 Jarm. Wills, 1455; Gray,

Perp. § 824; 6 Cyc. 615.

SUMMARY 

1. From a very early date, an estate for years could be created in a chattel real by sub-lease. 2 P. & M. Hist. 

Eng. Law, 112. 

2. The only interest which could be created by will in a chattel real, or by will or deed in chattel personal, in the beginning, was an absolute one. "A gift or devise of a chattel is forever."-Bro. Abr. Devise, 13. 

3. But the use and occupation of a chattel could be given for life, and such gift, while conveying no legal right of property, did give a right of enjoyment. Fitz-James's 

Case, p. 54. 

4. Early in the seventeenth century it became established that a bequest of a chattel real to A. for life, and on his death to B., was valid,—the whole term being regarded as vesting in A. until death, and then passing, by way of executory limitation, to B. Manning's Case, p. 55. 

5. During the latter half of the century, a similar bequest of a chattel personal also became recognized as valid; but upon the theory that A. has merely the use and occupation, and B. the immediate vested property. Hide v. Parrat, p. 57. 

According to modern English text-books, A. has the property in chattel, and B.'s interest is an executory bequest. Accord., Re Tritton, p. 60. (a) On either theory, at A.'s death B. has a legal right to possession. Hoare v. Parker, p. 59; Rogers v. Randall, p. 62. 

6. While a gift after a life interest in a term is valid by will (4, supra), it is said that no such gift can be made inter vivos. See Wright v. Cartwright, p. 58. 

Secus, it seems, in America. Ibid., note (b). (a) But a grant in futuro for so many years as remain at the death of one to whom the term is given for so long of it as he should live, is valid. Ibid.

7. While a gift after a life interest in a chattel personal is valid by will (5, supra), in England it is said that no such gift can be made inter vivos. Wms. Pers. Prop. 356. 

But in the United States (except in North Carolina) gifts after a life interest have been repeatedly upheld; and (except in North Carolina, Kentucky and Missouri), where the prior interest has been, not for life, but absolute, gifts over, necessarily executory, have also been allowed inter vivos. See Duke v. Dyches, p. 61, note; 

Wilson v. Cockrill, p. 62, note. It follows that in this country, a gift by deed of a chattel personal to take effect after the death of the donor is valid. Duke v. Dyches, supra. As is a parol gift to A. for life, and at his death to 

B., where delivery accompanies the gift to the first taker. 

Brummet v. Barber, p. 61. 

8. Since a bequest of a chattel real to A. for life passes the entire interest (4, supra), it would seem, in absence of limitation over, that the property should pass, on A.'s death, to his executor. But in the only case in point the executor of the testator was held entitled. Eyres v. 

Faulkland, p. 57. 

9. Since, under the early theory (5, supra), a bequest of a chattel personal to A. for life gives but the use and occupation, it would seem, in absence of a gift over, that the property should revert, on A.'s death, to the executor of the testator. This is the American view. Anonymous, p. 60. Under the modern English theory that A. has the property (5, supra), A.'s executor, on the other hand, should take. But the point appears undecided in Eng land. Gray, Perp. §§ 842, 854, k.

CHAPTER VI  SECTION I.-IN GENERAL INTRODUCTION The Statute of Wills, 32 Henry VIII. c. 1 (1540), in general terms, authorized the disposal of land by “last will and testament." The power of devise, so created, was for the most part untrammelled by the restrictions governing the transfer of property at common law. Thus, limitations of future estates by will were not subject to the common-law rules relating to abeyance of the seizin, the immediate freehold, during any unappropriated interval, being regarded as in the heir-at-law, to whom the entire estate would have passed in absence of devise. Nor did the rule prohibiting the limitation of a future interest after or in defeasance of a fee, apply to devises; for, in analogy to the principles governing the creation of future uses, it was decided that a devise could be made to take effect upon the determination, or in defeasance of a prior estate in fee.¹

These future limitations by will, which would not have been valid as limitations under the rules of the common law, became known as executory devises.2 It follows 

¹ Challis, Real Prop. 169, 170; 1 Leake, 50; 1 Tiffany, Real Prop. § 135. * In many points, the law as to executory devises and springing and shifting uses is the same. "Executory devises owed their origin to the doctrine of shifting and springing uses. But uses differ from executory devises in this respect; that there must be a person seized to the uses when the con- 5 65

that "every devise of a future interest, which is not preceded by an estate of freehold, created by the same will, or which, being so preceded, is limited to take effect before or after, and not at the expiration of such prior estate of freehold, is an executory devise.” ³ And since there can be no remainders in chattels, either real or personal, every future bequest, whether preceded by a particular interest or not, is necessarily of an executory nature. 4

With certain exceptions, the law as to executory devises and executory bequests is the same. tingency happens, or they cannot be executed by the statute. If the estate of the feoffee to such uses be destroyed by alienation or otherwise, before the contingency arises, the use is destroyed forever; whereas, by an executory devise, the freehold is transferred to the future devisee.”—4 Kent, Comm. 295.

An executory devise or a future use which, upon taking effect, cuts short another granted estate, is commonly known as a conditional limitation. 

Gray, Perp. §§ 32, 54. 

32 Jarm. Wills, 1432. To avoid confusion, it is to be remembered that the term executory is also used in a larger sense to denote all non-vested future interests, and so used, includes contingent remainders. For a disission of these several meanings of the word, see Gray, Perp. §§ 793–801. 

42 Jarm. Wills, 1453; Gray, Perp. § 321. 

"See preceding chapter.

CASES PELLS V. BROWN. 1620. (Cro. Jac. 590.) 

Facts: Devise to A. and his heirs forever; and if A. should die without issue, living B., then to B. and his heirs. A. enters; and, after suffering a common recovery to the use of himself and his heirs, devises the land to C. 

A. dies without issue in the lifetime of B. 

Question: Is B. barred by the recovery?

Held: Since the devise to A. is in fee, the limitation over must be an executory devise; for a fee cannot be limited after a fee by way of remainder. Taking under an executory devise, B. is not barred by the recovery, and may enter on the death of A. without issue.

Proposition: An executory devise is indestructible." 

GORE v. GORE. 1722. (2 P. Wms. 28.) 

The testator, seized in fee, devised to trustees for 500 years, remainder to the first and other sons of A. in tail (A. having no son at the testator's death), remainder over. 

Question: Is the limitation to the first son of A. valid?

Held: The devise is good, although preceded by an estate for years. 

Proposition: A future contingent devise is good, although limited after an estate for years." Lewis, Perp. 109. (a) For the importance of this decision in the development of the Rule against Perpetuities, see pp. 194, 195, infra. (b) The proposition that an executory devise is indestructible is, of course, subject to the reservation that all limitations expectant on an estate tail are destructible. Lewis, Perp. 133, 134; 4 Kent, Comm. 13. 

7 Of future devises,-as of future uses and future interests in personalty

DOE d. HERBERT v. SELBY. 1824. (2 B. & C. 926.) 

Facts: Devise to A. for life, and after his death to his children in fee; but if A. should die without issue, or, leaving issue, such issue should die under twenty-one without issue, then to B. After the testator's death, A. suffered a common recovery to the use of himself in fee, conveyed the premises to C., and died without issue. 

Question: Is B. barred by the recovery?

Held: Here the estate is given over on either of two contingencies: (1) A. dying without issue; or (2) A. leaving issue, and such issue dying under twenty-one without issue. If A. had left a child, a defeasible fee would have vested in that child, and the devise over could have operated only as an executory devise. But A. having died without having had a child, the limitation over could not have operated other than as a contingent remainder, and was accordingly defeated by the recovery.

Proposition: An estate may be devised over in either of two events, in one of which the devise may operate as a contingent remainder, and in the other as an executory devise.8 

SECTION II.-FAILURE OF EXECUTORY LIMITATION HARRISON V. FOREMAN. 1800. (5 Ves. 207.) 

Facts: Bequest of a fund to A. for life, and after her death to B. and C. in equal shares; and in case of the death of either in the lifetime of A., the whole to go to (see pp. 53, 54, supra)—substantially the only restraint on the creation, is the Rule against Perpetuities. Gray, Perp. §§ 55, 98; and see pp. 199, 200, infra. 

See 2 Jarm. Wills, 1450.

the survivor living at her decease. B. and C. both die in 

A.'s lifetime.

Question: Who is entitled to the fund on the death of A?

Held: By the terms of the will, B. and C. acquired vested interests in the fund. Where an interest once vests, the court will never permit it to be defeated, unless the very event has happened, in which it is declared, that interest shall not take effect. The contingency here expressed is, that if either B. or C. should die in A.'s lifetime, the whole is to go to the survivor. Neither was living at A.'s death. Consequently, not having been divested, their interests pass to their respective representatives.

Proposition: Once an interest under a devise or bequest has vested, the Court will not permit it to be divested unless it is perfectly clear that the very contingency has happened upon which divesting was to occur. 

JACKSON V. NOBLE. 1838. (2 Keen, 590.) 

Facts: Devise to A. for life, and after her death to her heirs and assigns: but in case A. should marry and have no children, then the property to belong to B.; or, in the event of his death in A.'s lifetime, then to his children. 

B. died childless in the life of A., who, at the time of the action, was married, but had no children. 

Question: What are A.'s rights?

Held: A. took an absolute interest, under the will, subject to be defeated in the event of her marrying and dying without issue in the lifetime of B., or of such child or children as B. might happen to leave. B. having died

childless in the life of A., neither contingency can occur, and A. is entitled to the property. 

Proposition: Once an interest under a devise or bequest has vested, the Court will not permit it to be divested unless it is perfectly clear that the very event has happened upon which divesting was to occur. 

DoE d. BLOMFIELD V. EYRE. 1848. (5 C. B. 713.) 

of

Facts: On the marriage of A., property is settled to the use of her husband for life, and after his death to the use the children of the marriage as A. should appoint. 

Having two sons, A. appoints to B., the elder, in fee, but if both should die before her husband, then to her fatherin-law (a stranger to the power) in fee. Both sons died in the lifetime of the husband. 

Question: Is B.'s estate defeated,-the appointment over to the father-in-law being void?

Held: By the appointment, an estate in fee vested in B., the elder son, defeasible in the event of both sons dying in their father's lifetime. This very contingency having occurred, B.'s estate is defeated, even though the appointment over is void. 

Proposition: An absolute appointment to an object of the power, with an executory gift over in a certain event, will cease on the happening of the event, even though the gift over be incapable of taking effect. Sugd. Pow. 514. • While this case has been criticized as irreconcilable with the doctrine that limitations are to be construed strictly against the divesting of vested estates (see Gray, Perp. § 786), its authority was reluctantly acknowledged in Robinson v. Wood, post, and Hurst v. Hurst, 21 Ch. D. 278, and it accordingly seems to be law in England. Theob. Wills, 627. In America, the rule is contra. Drummond's Executor v. Drummond, 26 N. J. Eq. 234; 

North Adams First Universalist Soc. v. Boland, 155 Mass. 171; 1 Tiffany:

Real Prop. § 148.

ROBINSON v. WOOD. 1858. (27 L. J. Ch. 726.) 

Facts: Devise to trustees upon trust to convey to A. upon attaining twenty-one, but if A. should die under twenty-one without issue her surviving, then to sell the property and pay the proceeds to the Primitive Methodist Society. The gift over is void under the Statute of Mortmain.

Question: A. having died under twenty-one without leaving issue, is her heir-at-law entitled to the property? 

Held: Assuming that a fee vested in A., it was subject to divestment in an event which has happened, viz. her dying under twenty-one without leaving issue. Therefore, under the rule of Blomfield v. Eyre, the estate must be held divested, even though the gift over is void. It follows that A.'s heir-at-law is not entitled. 

Proposition: If property is devised to A., with an executory gift over in a certain event to B., A.'s interest will be divested on the happening of the event, even though the gift over is incapable of taking effect.10 

O'MAHONEY V. BURDETT. 1874. (L. R. 7 H. L. 388.) 

Facts: Bequest to A., but if A. should die unmarried or without children, then to B. B. died in the lifetime of the testatrix; subsequently A. died without ever having had a child. 

Question: Is A.'s representative entitled?

Held: The very contingency upon which it was to be divested having occurred, A.'s estate must be held de- 

10 But where the gift over is upon a too remote event, the preceding estate continues. Brattle Square Church v. Grant, 3 Gray, 142; Sugd. Pow. 

514; 2 Jarm. Wills, 1437; Lewis, Perp. 657; 1 Tiffany, Real Prop. § 148.

feated, even though the gift over has lapsed. The residuary legatee takes the property. 

Proposition: On a bequest to A., with an executory gift over in a certain event to B., A.'s interest will be divested on the happening of the event, even though B. dies before the testator. Theob. Wills, 627.

SECTION III.-FAILURE OF PRECEDING LIMITATION JONES v. WESTCOMB. 1711. (1 Eq. Cas. Ab. 245, Pl. 10.) 

Facts: The testator, possessed of a long term for years, bequeathed it to his wife for life, and after her death to the child with which she was then enceinte; but if such child died under twenty-one, then over. The wife was not enceinte. 

Question: Did the gift over take effect?

Held: Although the wife was not enceinte, the gift over took effect.

Proposition: On a gift over in the event of the death under twenty-one "of the child with which A. is enceinte," the contingency expressed, by necessary implication, includes the contingency of there being no such child in esse; and the gift over will be sustained in the latter event.¹¹ 

11 Where a preceding interest fails because of the occurrence of a contingency not expressly provided for, the gift over will be sustained if it clearly appears that the testator must a fortiori (as in the principal case) have intended it to take effect in the event which has happened. But the court cannot make a will for the testator; and if no such intention is manifest, it is powerless to sustain the gift over, even though it is probable, could the testator be consulted, that he would say it was to have effect equally in either event. 2 Jarm. Wills, 2195 seq.; Theobald, Wills, 629. 630.

WILLING V. BAINE. 1731. (3 P. Wms. 113.) 

Facts: A. bequeaths £200 to each of his children, payable at twenty-one; and if any die under twenty-one, that share to go to the survivors. One of the children dies under twenty-one in A.'s lifetime.

Question: Are the surviving children entitled to the legacy, or must it be deemed to have lapsed?

Held: The surviving children are entitled, even though the event upon which the legacy was given over to them happened in the testator's lifetime. 

Proposition: If there is a bequest to A., with a gift over on his death under twenty-one, and A. dies under that age in the lifetime of the testator, the gift over takes effect.12 Theob. Wills, 631.

AVELYN v. WARD. 1750. (1 Ves. Sr. 420.) 

Facts: Devise to A. in fee, on condition that within three months after the testator's death, he give a general release of all claims against the estate; but if he neglect to do so, the property to go to B. A. dies before the testator. 

Question: Does the gift over take effect?

Held: Since it fairly appears from the will that if the precedent limitation, by what means soever, is out of the case, the subsequent limitation is to have effect, B. is entitled to the property.

Proposition: Upon a devise to A., with a gift over on his failing to perform a condition, even though the death 12 But if A. dies above the age in the testator's life, the gift over does not take effect. See Doo v. Brabant, 3 B. C. C. 393; also note, p. 74, infra.

of A. in the testator's life renders performance impossible, the gift over takes effect. Theob. Wills, 630. 

TARBUCK V. TARBUCK. 1835. (4 L. J. N. S. Ch. 129.) 

Facts: Devise to A. for life, remainder to his children in fee; but if he should die without leaving issue, then over. A. died in the life of the testator, leaving a son, who also predeceased the testator. 

Question: Does the devise over take effect?

Held: The devise over is dependent upon the death of 

A. without leaving issue. Since A. died leaving issue, this very contingency can never happen; and the gift over consequently cannot take effect, even though the prior gift has lapsed. 

Proposition: If all conditions are fulfilled entitling prior devisees to absolute interests, supposing they had survived the testator, a devise over will not take effect merely because the prior gift has lapsed. 13 Theob. Wills, 632.

HUGHES V. ELLIS. 1855. (20 Beav. 193.) 

Facts: Bequest to the testator's wife A., her executors, administrators and assigns; but if A. should die intestate, then to B. A. dies intestate in the life of the testator. 

13 "There is, it is submitted, a solid difference between sustaining a devise which is to take effect in the event of a person not in esse dying under a certain age, though such person never come into existence, and holding it to take effect in the event of his being born and dying above that age in the lifetime of the testator. . . . To let in the ulterior devise in such case would be to give the estate to one, in the very event in which the testator has declared that it shall go to another, whose incapacity, by reason of death, to take, seems to form no solid ground for changing its object. In the event which has happened, the lapsed devise must be read as an absolute gift."-2 Jarm. Wills, 2203.

Question: Is B. entitled? 

Held: The word "intestate" renders it impossible to sustain the gift over as a provision against lapse, since

A. (a feme covert) could not have died other than intestate in the testator's lifetime. The only reasonable construction is that the gift over contemplates A. surviving the testator. But if A. had survived, she would have taken an absolute interest, and the direction in the will seeking to control its descent in event of intestacy, could not have prevailed. Therefore, being incapable of taking effect if A. had survived the testator, the gift over cannot take effect in the event which has happened. 

Proposition: A limitation over, incapable of taking effect had the prior legatee survived the testator, cannot take effect on the lapse of the prior gift.¹4

SUMMARY SECTION I.-IN GENERAL 

1. An executory devise is indestructible. Pells v. Brown, p. 67. 

2. A future contingent devise is valid, although preceded by an estate for years. Gore v. Gore, p. 67. 

3. An estate may be devised over in either of two events, in one of which the devise may operate as a contingent remainder, and in the other as an executory devise. 

Doe d. Herbert v. Selby, p. 68. 

14 If personalty is bequeathed to A. and the heirs of his body, and if he die without issue to B., this is an absolute gift to A. if he survives the testator, and the gift over is void. But if A dies without issue in the testator's lifetime, B. takes,-the gift over being validated by lapse of the prior gift. In re Lowman, [1895] 2 Ch. 348. The principal case, so far as inconsistent, is overruled by the later holding. Theob. Wills, 623; 1 Jarm. 

Wills, 452; Gray, Perp. § 231, note.

SECTION II.-FAILURE OF EXECUTORY LIMITATION 

1. Once an interest under a devise or bequest has vested, the Court will not permit it to be divested unless it is perfectly clear that the very event has happened upon which divesting was to occur. Harrison v. Foreman, p. 68; 

Jackson v. Noble, p. 69. 

2. If, however, the very contingency does happen, the previously vested interest will be divested. (a) Even though the gift over be void. Doe d. Blomfield v. Eyre, p. 70; Robinson v. Wood, p. 71. Unless the objection thereto is remoteness. P. 71, note. (b) Even though the legatee to take under the gift over die in the testator's lifetime. O'Mahoney v. Burdett, p. 71.

SECTION III.-FAILURE OF PRECEDING LIMITATION 

1. On the failure of a preceding limitation because of the occurrence of a contingency not in terms provided for, a gift over will be sustained if the contingency expressed, by necessary implication, includes the event which has happened. (a) Thus, a gift over in the event of the child "which A. is enceinte of" failing to reach a certain age, will be sustained in the event of there being no such child in esse. Jones v. 

Westcomb, p. 72. (b) As will a gift over in the event of a prior devisee failing to perform a certain condition, where the death of the devisee in the life of the testator renders performance impossible. Avelyn v. Ward, p. 73. 

2. If there is a gift to A., with a limitation over on his death under twenty-one to B., if A. dies under twenty-one in the testator's lifetime, B. takes. Willing v. Baine, p. 73. Otherwise, if A. die above the age. Doo v. Brabant, p. 73, note.

. 

3. Upon similar principles, on a gift to the children of 

A., but if A. should die without issue, then to B., if A. dies in the testator's lifetime leaving issue who also predecease the testator, the gift over fails. Tarbuck v. Tarbuck, p. 74. 

4. If personalty is bequeathed to A. and the heirs of his body, and in case of the failure of issue of A., then to 

B., if A. dies without issue in the testator's life, B. takes, although he would have taken nothing had A. survived. 

Re Lowman, p. 75, note; contra, Hughes v. Ellis, p. 74.

CHAPTER VII CROSS LIMITATIONS SECTION I.-WHEN CROSS LIMITATIONS IMPLIED INTRODUCTION Cross Remainders: Cross remainders arise when con current particular estates are limited to two or more persons, and on the termination of the interest of any such person, his share is to remain over to the tenants of all the particular estates still existent, for such time as each of their estates continues, so that the ultimate taker is not let into possession until the determination of all the particular estates.¹ A simple illustration of cross remainders is afforded where land is limited, as to one undivided moiety, to A. in tail with remainder to B. in tail, and as to the other undivided moiety, to B. in tail with remainder to A. in tail. In a deed, cross remainders can be created only by express words.2 In a devise, they may be implied from the context.3

Cross Executory Limitations: Cross executory limita tions arise when, after absolute interests are limited to two or more persons, it is provided that, in certain events, the share of each shall pass to the other or others. In their general aspect, cross executory limitations are similar to 

11 Prest. Est. 96; Challis, Real Prop. 371; 1 Tiffany, Real Prop. § 118. 4 

2 Cole v. Levingston, 1 Vent. 224; Doe v. Dorvell, 5 T. R. 518. 'Atkinson v. Barton, 3 D. F. & J. 339, 346; 1 Jarm. Wills, 660; Challis, Real Prop. 373. 

* 1 Jarm. Wills, 669; 1 Tiffany, Real Prop. § 145. 

78

CROSS LIMITATIONS 

79 cross remainders. But cross executory limitations in derogation of vested absolute interests are never raised by implication. For cross limitations are only implied to avoid a partial intestacy, which would otherwise result from a seemingly unintentional gap in the limitations: and where the testator's whole estate or interest vests under the primary gift, it continues vested in every event save that upon which it is given over; and, consequently, in no case can there be a partial intestacy.5 

CASES SCOTT v. BARGEMAN. 1722. (2 P. Wms. 68.) 

Facts: Bequest of £900 to trustees upon trust "to divide the same equally among my three daughters A., B. and 

C., at their respective ages of twenty-one; provided, if all three die before their legacies become payable, then the £900 to be paid to their mother." A. and B., the two eldest daughters, die under twenty-one. C. subsequently attains majority. 

Question: Is C. entitled to the entire £900?

Held: The bequest contains no gift to the daughters other than in a direction for payment, and such direction attaches only upon a person of the age of twenty-one. 

As A. and B. died under that age, it cannot be said that their shares had vested, so as to devolve upon their representatives. Nor can it be said that the shares which 'Skey v. Barnes, p. 81, infra; In re Hudson, 20 Ch. Div. 406; Theob. 

Wills, 710, 711; 1 Tiffany, Real Prop. § 145. But after contingent absolute gifts, cross limitations may be implied. Scott v. Bargeman, post; Theob. 

Wills, 711; 1 Jarm. Wills, 670. • While the holding that the shares of A. and B. had not vested at death is placed upon a different ground in the report, it is submitted that that above stated is the only theory upon which the holding can be sustained. 

See Skey v. Barnes, 3 Mer. 342; Graves v. Waters, 10 Ir. Eq. 234.

A. and B. would have received, had they attained majority, were undisposed of so as to sink into the residue; for, at their death, there was still a possibility that C., the youngest daughter, would also die under twenty-one, in which event the entire £900 would pass to the mother. 

Therefore, since the mother was to be entitled to the whole if all died under twenty-one, and to nothing if any survived that age, survivorship among the children seems to be implied, though not expressly provided for. 

Proposition: If a bequest be to A., B. and C. in equal shares at their respective ages of twenty-one, with a gift over of the entire property on the death of all under that age, on the death of any under twenty-one during the infancy of the others, a gift of his share to the survivors will be raised by implication. 

ARMSTRONG v. ELDRIDGE. 1791. (3 Bro. C. C. 215.) 

Facts: A. devises property to trustees upon trust to apply the interest equally among his granddaughters, B., C., D. and E., for their respective lives, and, after the death of the survivor, to divide the principal among all and every the children of said granddaughters, share and share alike. B. and C. die leaving issue.

Question: What becomes of the interest which B. and C. took until the death of the surviving granddaughter?

Held: Although cross limitations are not expressly provided for, it is evident that the whole interest is to be divided among four granddaughters while four are living, among three, while three are living, etc., and that nothing is to go to the children, while any of the mothers are living. 

It follows the whole interest vests in D. and E. by survivorship.

CROSS LIMITATIONS 

81 Proposition: Where income is given A. and B. during their lives in equal shares, with a gift over of the principal after the death of the survivor, the survivor takes the whole income for life. Theob. Wills, 497.

DoE d. GORGES v. WEBB. 1808. (1 Taunt. 234.) 

Facts: The will provides: "I give the said moiety of the said premises to my three daughters A., B., and C., and to the heirs of their bodies respectively, as tenants in common; and in default of such issue, I give the same to my own right heirs forever."

Question: Are cross remainders implied after the termination of the respective estates tail in each of the tenants in common, by failure of her issue?

Held: The fact that the same property as is devised to 

A., B. and C. in tail as tenants in common, is limited over on failure of their issue, shows that nothing was intended to go over, unless all went. Cross remainders will, therefore, be implied to effectuate the object of the devise. 

Proposition: Under a devise to several as tenants in common in tail, with a limitation over in default of such issue, cross remainders are implied after the termination of the respective estates tail in each, by the failure of his issue. 1 Tiffany, Real Prop. § 126. 

SKEY V. BARNES. 1816. (3 Mer. 335.) 

Facts: Bequest of a fund upon trust for A. for life, and after her death for her children equally, the share of each to be paid on his or her respectively attaining twenty-one; but in the event of all such children dying without issue before their respective portions are payable, then to B. 

A. is survived by several children, of whom C. dies un-

married under the age of twenty-one. The others attain majority.

Question: Who is entitled to C.'s share, the surviving children or C.'s representatives?

Held: Under the bequest the shares immediately vested, although payment was deferred until attainment of the prescribed age. The shares so vesting were subject to divestment in the event of all the children dying under twenty-one, without issue. As that very contingency has not occurred, they have not been divested; and the share of C. passed to her representative. 

Proposition: Where absolute vested interests are bequeathed to several as tenants in common, with a limitation over upon the death of all in certain events, cross limitations will not be implied, because in no case can there be a partial intestacy."

JONES v. RANDALL. 1819. (1 Jac. & W. 100.) 

Facts: Bequest of an annuity to the children of A. living at her death, to be divided among them in equal shares and proportions. "Such annuity to be paid during the lives of the children of A., and the life of the survivor of them." 

At A.'s death, four children are living, of whom B. dies in the lifetime of the others. 

7 "In the case of a devise to several persons in tail, assuming the intention to be clear that the estate is not to go over to the remainderman until all the devisees shall have died without issue, the effect of not implying crossremainders among the tenants in tail would be to produce a chasm in the limitations, inasmuch as some of the estates tail might be spent, while the ulterior devise could not take effect until the failure of all. On the other hand, in the case of limitations in fee of the realty, and of absolute interests in personalty (both of which are clearly governed by the same principle), as the primary gift includes the testator's whole estate or interest, and that interest remains in the objects in every event upon which it is not divested, a partial intestacy can never arise for want of a limitation over.”—1 Jarm. 

Wills, 669.

CROSS LIMITATIONS 

83 Question: Who is entitled to B.'s share, his representative or the surviving children?

Held: The gift of the annuity to all the children of A. who should survive her, in equal shares and proportions, creates a tenancy in common. The mere fact that the gift is to continue during the lives of the survivors will not raise an inference that the remaining children are entitled by survivorship to the share of the deceased child, in the face of the express words of tenancy in common. Hence, B.'s share passes to his representative. 

Proposition: On a bequest of an annuity to several persons in terms which constitute a tenancy in common, the interests of the annuitants will not be varied merely by reason of the annuity being given "for their lives and the life of the survivor"; and upon the death of each annuitant his share will pass to his representative until the death of the survivor.

ASHLEY V. ASHLEY. 1833. (6 Sim. 358.) 

Facts: Devise of property to all and every the children of A., equally to be divided among them, as tenants in common, and "for want of such issue," to B. A. had eight children, five of whom died without issue.

Question: Who is entitled to the property of the deceased children?

Held: The property is given to the children of A. as tenants in common, and for want of such issue to B. The expression "for want of such issue" means either a want of issue by there being no children originally, or by such children ceasing to exist; and indicates that the property is not to pass to B. until after the death of all. The children, therefore, take estates for life as tenants in

common, with cross remainders between them for life; and the shares of the deceased children pass to the survivors for life.

Proposition: A devise to the children of A. for life, and for want of such issue over, creates cross remainders by implication for life among such devises. 1 Jarm. Wills, 668.

DRAYCOTT v. WOOD. 1863. (8 L. T. N. S. 304.) 

Facts: Devise of a residue to trustees to divide the income therefrom equally among A., B., C., D. and E. for their respective lives, and after their death, to pay the same to F. A. dies. 

Question: Who is entitled to the share of A?

Held: The share of A. passes to the survivors, the presumed intention being that the property should continue in mass, passing through the hands of those to whom lifeinterests are given, to the ultimate taker, as a whole. 

Proposition: Where an annuity is given A., B. and C., to be equally divided among them for their lives, with a gift over of the annuity as a whole after the death of the survivor, on the death of any, his share passes to the survivors for life. Theob. Wills, 497.

CROSS LIMITATIONS 

85 

SUMMARY 

1. Cross limitations are only implied to avoid a partial intestacy, which would otherwise result from a seemingly unintentional hiatus in the limitations. In re Hudson, 20 

Ch. Div. 406, 415. 

Such a gap may occur: (a) Where there is a devise to the life, and for want of such children, over. p. 83. children of A. for 

Ashley v. Ashley, (b) Where there is a bequest to several as tenants in common for their respective lives, with a gift over after the death of the survivor. Armstrong v. Eldridge, p. 80; 

Draycott v. Wood, p. 84. (c) Where there is a devise to several in tail, being tenants in common, with a gift over on failure of issue of all. Doe d. Gorges v. Webb, p. 81. (d) Where the entire property is given to several as tenants in common, and the interests are contingent, with a gift over in the event of the death of all before vesting. 

Scott v. Bargeman, p. 79. 

2. But cross limitations will not be implied where no such gap can occur, and the only effect of implication would be the divesting of vested interests. In re Hudson, ubi supra. (a) Thus, upon a bequest of an annuity to several as tenants in common, "for their lives and the life of the survivor," the share of each annuitant passes at death to his representative until the death of the survivor. Jones v. Randall, p. 82. (b) Similarly, upon a gift of the entire property to several as tenants in common, with a limitation over on death of all in certain events, if the interests under the primary gift are vested, cross limitations will not be implied, since in no case can there be a partial intestacy. 

Skey v. Barnes, p. 81.

SECTION II.-WHEN "SURVIVOR" CONSTRUED AS 

"OTHER" 

INTRODUCTION 

A question of implication of cross limitations, slightly different from that discussed in the preceding section, arises where the testator has attempted express cross limitations, and in order to carry out his intention it is necessary to enlarge upon the literal meaning of his words. Thus, on a limitation to A., B. and C. for life, with remainder to their issue, and if any die without issue to the survivors for life, with remainder to their issue, and if all die without issue, then over, there can be little doubt, if A. should die leaving issue, and B. afterwards die without issue, but that the testator intended the issue of A. to take, if living at the death of B.; and yet, unless "survivors" be read as "others," this intention cannot prevail. The question as to what circumstances will warrant such reading of the word "survivors" is the subject of the present section. In some of the earlier cases, it seemed to be assumed that in gifts to families the word "survivors" ex vi termini must mean "others." But the modern authorities are agreed that there is no such rule of law, and that, unless a contrary intention appear from the context, "survivors" must be construed according to its plain natural meaning, like any other expression. 10 

8 See note, p. 87, infra. 

9 Barlow v. Salter, 17 Ves. 482; Beckwith v. Beckwith, p. 90 infra, 45 L. 

J. Ch. N. S. 99. 

10 Ferguson v. Dunbar, p. 88, infra; Beckwith v. Beckwith, supra;

Milsom v. Awdry, p. 89, infra; Inderwick v. Tatchell, [1903] A. C. 120; 

Carter v. Bloodgood's Executors, 3 Sandf. Ch. (N. Y.) 293, 299.

CASES HARMAN V. DICKENSON. 1781. (1 Bro. C. C. 91.) 

Facts: Bequest of £10,000 to trustees, to divide the same into two sums of £5,000 each, and to pay the income of one to A. and the other to B. for life; and on the death of each, to assign her £5,000 to her children; but if either should die without leaving issue, then to apply the £5,000, intended for the children of the one so dying, for the benefit of the survivor, for life, and after her death, to divide the same equally among her children; and if both should die without leaving issue, then to transfer the entire fund to C. A. dies leaving issue; subsequently B. dies without issue. The issue of A. are living at the time of the action. 

Question: Are the issue of A. entitled to the £5,000 given over to the children of the survivor, in the event of the death of either of the legatees without issue?

Held: The issue of A. are entitled, even though she did not survive.11

Proposition: If a devise or bequest be to several for life, with remainder to their issue, and if any die without issue, to the survivors for life, with remainder to their issue, 11 This holding, for which no reasons are assigned in the brief and inaccurate report, is sustainable upon the following grounds: (a) The gift over on the death of either of the legatees without leaving issue, to the survivor for life, and after her death to her children, would indicate an intention that so long as there were issue to take, they should enjoy the entire property, regardless as to whether their parent (whose interest in any event ceases at death) died first or last. (b) And where, in addition, we have an ultimate gift over on death of both without issue, the conclusion is ir resistible that cross limitations between the stocks were intended, irrespective of the periods at which the parents die. This intention can be effectuated only by reading “survivors" as "others." 2 Jarm. Wills, 2105; and see Beckwith v. Beckwith, p. 90, infra; Harrison v. Harrison, p. 91, infra.

with an ultimate gift over if all the tenants for life die without issue, then if a tenant for life dies leaving issue and another afterwards dies without issue and there are issue of the first living at the death of the second, such issue will take. 

Theob. Wills, 660.

FERGUSON v. DUNBAR. 1781. (3 Bro. C. C. 469n.) 

Facts: Bequest of a fund equally among B., C. and D., after the death of A, and if any die in the lifetime of A., leaving issue, that share to be divided among such issue; but if any die under twenty-one without issue, that share to go to the survivors; if all should die without issue, then the fund to fall into the residue. At A.'s death, B., C. and 

D. were living. B. died leaving issue. Subsequently C. died under twenty-one, and without issue. D. and the issue of B. were living at the bringing of the action. 

Question: Are the children of B. entitled to any portion of C.'s share; or, in other words, is "survivors" to be construed as "others"?

Held: It is probable that the testator intended the issue of B. to take the share which would have accrued to the parent, if living. However, not having said so, but having limited such share to the survivors instead, the whole of C.'s share must pass to D., as sole survivor. 

Proposition: Even though a limitation to surviving children contain a gift over in the event of the death of all without issue, "survivors" must be strictly construed, where the shares of the survivors are settled, not for life merely, with remainder to their issue, but absolutely instead, 12 

12 Twist v. Herbert, 28 L. T. N. S. 489; 2 Jarm. Wills, 2105, note (b).

Where there is a gift to several for life, with remainders to their issue, and if any die without issue, to the "survivors" and their issue, and if all die
